# GPT-OSS-120B optimization log

The append-only experiment record, including failed trials and cumulative accepted-stage history, is in [the research log](research/gpt_oss_120b_optimization_log.md). This document retains the detailed setup, profiler interpretation, and implementation tradeoffs.

This log records measurements, decisions, and tradeoffs for GPT-OSS-120B tensor-parallel inference in Garnet. The target is to exceed a current, optimized vLLM run on the same rented two-GPU machine without changing answers. A standalone kernel improvement is only a candidate until an end-to-end model run confirms it.

## Test method and hardware

- Machine: 2 × RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Max-Q (96 GB each), connected through PCIe/PHB, with 300 GB instance disk. This is tensor parallelism (TP2): each GPU holds part of the model, and both participate in each token.
- Run one server/benchmark at a time. Run vLLM first, then Garnet, with the same saved request IDs, prompt text, output caps, decoding settings, model weights, and BF16 KV cache. Record correctness for arithmetic, code tracing, instruction following, and long context.
- Separate cold first-request time (which can include one-time MXFP4 packing) from repeat prefill and warm decode. Report wall-clock decode including host updates and both ranks; a forward-only rate omits meaningful overhead. Keep raw logs and profiler traces in `D:/CantorAI/work/gpt-oss-benchmark-evidence-2026-10-05/` (outside Git).
- `tools/gpt_oss/run_tp_prefill.py` repeats the same TP2 request in one loaded process and records the first (potentially cold) pass separately from the subsequent warm passes; use it for prefill A/B runs with all other environment settings held constant.
- vLLM reference: 0.31.0, TP2, BF16 KV, FlashInfer attention, Marlin experts, and its automatic all-reduce selection. A different vLLM configuration would require a fresh baseline.

The user also requires **maximum aggregate output throughput under batching**. Sweep matched batch sizes (initially 1, 2, 4 and 8, stopping at the practical memory limit), input lengths (saved short and 2,005-token long prompts), and fixed output lengths (initially 128 and 512 tokens where context permits). Use greedy decoding with early EOS disabled on both engines, and keep the KV-cache dtype BF16 and maximum context matched. Run optimized vLLM first and Garnet second, one engine at a time. Record aggregate generated tokens per second across all requests from the earliest first token to the last completion, full-request throughput including prefill, per-request completion latency, output token IDs, GPU memory, KV allocation/capacity, and the highest stable batch size. Profile representative bottlenecks from this sweep instead of collecting a costly full Cartesian product. Report single-request latency separately; do not use a batch-1 result as evidence of maximum throughput. The dedicated runners are `tools/gpt_oss/run_vllm_batch_throughput.py` and `tools/gpt_oss/benchmark_batch_tp2.sh`. Their timing boundaries differ slightly at the host/API layer, so include raw timestamps and decode-step timings when interpreting close results.

For 4 × 2,005-token prefill, the grouped MoE fallback requests 759,916,596 bytes of TensorRT scratch, exceeding the former 256 MiB builder cap. The TP2 builder now estimates that plugin scratch from the combined input rows and rounds its workspace cap to 1 GiB for this shape; this affects engine construction, not the persistent KV allocation. It was verified against the exact failure and a successful Vast run. The shape remains slow: about 3.03 s prefill, versus a full vLLM batch request of about 1.09 s. The append-only research log records matched decode/full-request numbers and answer checks. The next prefill candidate should avoid the large grouped-MoE fallback or chunk the prompt while preserving paged-KV correctness; profile before choosing between those approaches.

For higher batch sizes, the benchmark runners accept `GARNET_BATCH_CONTEXT_CAPACITY` and `VLLM_BATCH_CONTEXT_CAPACITY` so both engines can be measured with the same smaller maximum context when the 4,096-token KV reservation would exceed Garnet's memory budget. Keep input plus output within that context and compare only pairs with the same capacity; the reduced-context results are a separate series from the 4,096-token profile.

## October 6 rerun setup

The original Japan instance 54383358 could not regain its two GPUs after restart and stayed `Scheduling`. It is now stopped as a backup; its 300 GB disk, with 281 GB used, costs about $0.084/hour. Vast accepted a copy request from that instance to the new one, but the source-side rsync failed (`No user exists for uid 0`), so no copied data has been accepted as valid.

The replacement is interruptible Vast instance 54530961 in Czechia: 2 × RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Max-Q (96 GB each), PCIe 5.0 ×16, 350 GB disk. The suggested bid at creation was $1.540/hour for GPUs plus $0.098/hour for storage ($1.638/hour total); the running instance subsequently displayed $1.617/hour for GPUs ($1.715/hour total). Interruptible bids can be outbid, so checkpoint results and stop the GPU when the experiment is finished. The host-reliability indicator was 95.4% when selected.

The new machine cloned Garnet `feature/gpt-oss-120b` at `947b357` and XLang3 at `990a745f` from GitHub. It downloaded the original GPT-OSS checkpoint (about 61 GB) and a separate Hugging Face-layout checkpoint for vLLM (about 61 GB). The Hugging Face download also included an unused 61 GB `metal/` directory, which was removed. This leaves substantially more build and engine-cache headroom than copying all 281 GB from the old disk into 350 GB. Setup uses the PyTorch NGC 26.08 image, CUDA 13.4, system TensorRT 11.2.1.2, and isolated vLLM 0.31.0 installation.

The new-machine vLLM 0.31.0 baseline has finished. It used TP2, BF16 KV, FlashInfer attention (XQA decode), Marlin MXFP4 experts, and its selected custom/PyNCCL all-reduce backends; no Garnet GPU process ran alongside it. Three trials per case, after an eight-token warmup, gave:

| Case | Warm decode tok/s (three trials) | Time to first token (three trials) |
|---|---|---|
| Arithmetic | 247.65, 247.63, 247.56 | 30.22, 30.11, 29.98 ms |
| Code tracing | 247.24, 247.25, 247.29 | 30.76, 30.98, 30.70 ms |
| Instruction following | 247.45, 247.36, 247.37 | 29.28, 29.74, 29.52 ms |
| Long context | 247.44, 247.99, 248.07 | 98.89, 100.20, 100.68 ms |

Raw vLLM results, configuration, and logs are saved under `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54530961/`. The measurements below are from the same Czechia machine; the later Japan table is historical and must not be treated as a same-host comparison.

### Czechia Garnet build and first measurements

TensorRT 11 removed `NetworkDefinitionCreationFlag::kEXPLICIT_BATCH`, so the local Garnet branch now passes zero for that flag on TensorRT 11 while retaining it on older versions (`01a08c3`). XLang3's VM-loop translation unit stalled for over 32 minutes at GCC `-O3`, and a separate `-O2` attempt also made little progress. Only that translation unit was built at `-O0` for initial correctness, then at `-O1` for the current performance runs; all other XLang3 objects remain Release `-O3`. This is a benchmark limitation, not a Garnet source change. The runtime's Python 3.14 library came from CPython's `v3.14.0` `Lib` directory; `PYTHONPATH` includes the Garnet build's `bin` directory. The full GPT-OSS correctness suite, including CUDA kernel parity, batched scalar updates, TP collectives, model graph, and Qwen compatibility, passed with this runtime.

On the 256-token arithmetic input, GCC `-O0` VM-loop objects gave **146.32 and 147.35 tok/s** warm wall decode in two runs. Switching only those objects to `-O1` raised the rate to **164.11 tok/s**. Batched scalar updates gave **165.83 tok/s** in a following trial. Each produced the expected final JSON answer. The first complete long-context run with tiled attention and batched updates gave **148.34 tok/s** warm wall decode and the expected answer. These are exploratory single-prompt measurements; vLLM remains near **248 tok/s** on the same hardware.

For the 2,005-token prompt, three prefill passes in one loaded process measured **0.619 s cold, 0.425/0.418 s warm** without tiled attention and **0.483 s cold, 0.280/0.265 s warm** with `GARNET_GPT_OSS_PREFILL_TILED_64=1`. Both paths gave the same first token (`200005`), and the tiled full answer passed. The first full tiled request measured 0.483 s prefill because it also included one-time packing. vLLM's ~0.100 s time to first token has a different timing boundary, but the remaining gap is substantial.

The node-level Nsight Systems decode trace captured 88 CUDA graph replays on each GPU. Median graph duration was about **4.72 ms**. In a steady ~64-token window on GPU 0, Marlin experts totaled about **0.95 ms/token**, TensorRT GEMMs **1.16 ms/token**, direct reductions **0.56 ms/token**, and split attention **0.35 ms/token**; metadata, routing, norms, and casts account for more. The wall rate near 166 tok/s is about 6.0 ms/token, so host updates, rank synchronization, sampling, and graph-launch gaps also matter. The raw trace is `work/garnet-o1-profile/arithmetic-node.nsys-rep` on the Vast machine. These groups are summed kernel durations per GPU, not additive shares of wall time.

One grouped attention configuration screen on the arithmetic request passed final-answer checks for all variants: split-8 **162.22**, split-32 **166.30**, one-kernel 16-warp **152.85**, and one-kernel 8-warp **138.27 tok/s**. The default split-16 with batched updates was **165.83 tok/s**, so the split-32 difference is too small to justify a default change. Output token counts differed across variants even though the final JSON was correct. Blackwell Marlin CTA overrides gave **164.97 tok/s** at two CTAs/SM and **152.59** at four versus the one-CTA/SM default near 166; retain the default.

The next grouped candidate (`0a5e1cb`) fuses decode-time Marlin input conversion into router-score work and expert metadata into top-K routing, removing two graph launches per MoE layer. It is opt-in as `GARNET_GPT_OSS_FUSED_DECODE_ROUTE=1`. CUDA parity exercised both unsharded and two-rank Marlin with the option, and the full correctness suite passed with tiled prefill and batched controls enabled. The four-request, two-trial end-to-end run was interrupted while building/loading the first TP2 prefill engines: Vast marked Czechia instance 54530961 **Outbid** and SSH became unavailable. No fused-route end-to-end speed or answer result was collected, so the fusion remains unaccepted. The user subsequently destroyed both the Czechia and Japan instances; Vast showed zero instances and no retained-storage charge. Local source, saved prompts, and vLLM results remain, but the remote checkpoint, engine cache, and any trace copied only to Vast must be recreated. Re-run the saved sequential benchmark on an available GPU before deciding whether to keep the option.

For the next on-demand run, the user selected Vast offer **#54377895** in British Columbia from the October 6 listing. It advertises two 96 GB RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPUs, PCIe 5.0 x16 at 54.1 GB/s, 193 GB assigned host RAM, 1,969 MB/s disk, and 93.7% reported reliability. With a 400 GB container, the displayed price is $2.534/hour GPU plus $0.112/hour disk = **$2.645/hour**, excluding usage-based bandwidth ($4/TB upload, $2.667/TB download). Vast created on-demand instance **54543362** on host 622130 at about 21:17 UTC. SSH verified two **NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Workstation Edition** GPUs (97,887 MiB each, compute capability 12.0), TensorRT 11.2.1.2, and an initially empty 400 GB disk. The user raised the initial $140 total-spend limit to **$250**. At the listed rate, stop the GPU by roughly 90 hours after creation (sooner when experiments finish or charges approach the cap), leaving about $12 for bandwidth; the initial account credit of $160.39 will require replenishment before the full authorized time. A stopped 400 GB disk continues to cost $0.112/hour. The 400 GB allocation exceeds the previous Japan instance's approximately 281 GB peak use and leaves room for the two 61 GB checkpoint layouts, build outputs, and engine caches. Garnet and XLang3 were cloned from GitHub at `c299b01` and `990a745f` using temporary read-only deploy keys. Recreate weights and caches, check latest stable vLLM (0.31.0 as of October 6), run its four saved requests first, then Garnet without a competing GPU inference process. Copy raw logs and profiler traces locally before releasing the instance.

The new Workstation Edition host built XLang3 Release with only its VM-loop translation unit at GCC `-O1` and all other units at `-O3`; Garnet and its GPT-OSS plugin built for SM 120 against CUDA 13.4 and system TensorRT 11.2.1.2. The complete synthetic GPT-OSS verification suite passed, including CUDA kernel parity, batched scalar control updates on both GPUs, compiled graph cold/warm paths, TP collectives, multi-GPU model generation, the native-module bridge, and Qwen compatibility. The two-rank TensorRT collective graph also passed. These checks do not establish full pretrained performance or answer correctness. `tools/gpt_oss/benchmark_tp2.sh` now provides a GPU-exclusive Linux runner for the four saved prompts and records raw output plus its configuration. The model layouts and isolated vLLM 0.31.0 environment were still being downloaded when these checks finished.

The two checkpoint layouts finished at about 61 GB each. A fresh vLLM 0.31.0 installation ran first, alone, with TP2, BF16 KV, greedy sampling, prefix caching disabled, chunked prefill, MXFP4 Marlin experts, FlashInfer XQA decode/prefill, and CUDA graphs. Its three warm trials per saved request measured:

| Workstation Edition case | Decode tok/s (three trials) | Time to first token (three trials) |
|---|---|---|
| Arithmetic | 276.60, 273.43, 273.34 | 27.37, 35.23, 36.12 ms |
| Code tracing | 272.62, 272.63, 272.60 | 35.13, 36.50, 36.46 ms |
| Instruction following | 272.38, 272.55, 272.53 | 33.80, 35.89, 35.66 ms |
| Long context | 272.53, 272.44, 272.77 | 115.25, 116.16, 116.39 ms |

The arithmetic first trial's faster rate and lower first-token time are visible in the raw data; use the repeated trials as the reference range. Output token counts varied for some cases despite temperature zero, so validate final answers separately rather than assuming byte-identical generations. Configuration, per-token arrival times, and console logs are saved locally under `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/vllm-benchmarks/`. Garnet runs started only after vLLM exited and `nvidia-smi` showed no compute processes.

The first Garnet full-checkpoint run enabled rank-local inputs, direct TP reduction, batched control copies, fused Marlin decode routing and lock clear, a 32-row Marlin prefill block, and sixteen-query tiled prefill. Its two trials per case passed all four expected final JSON answers. A second run changed only the control-update path: four scalar values were enqueued by one small GPU kernel on each rank's execution thread, ahead of that rank's model graph, instead of issuing four host-to-device copies and a stream synchronization per rank. It also passed every expected final answer. Both runs used the same cached TensorRT engines, model weights, token IDs, and output caps:

| Workstation Edition case | Garnet batched-copy decode tok/s | Garnet inline-kernel decode tok/s | Inline-kernel prefill |
|---|---|---|---|
| Arithmetic | 190.90, 189.27 | 193.84, 192.86 | 0.209, 0.212 s |
| Code tracing | 190.61, 190.78 | 195.86, 194.78 | 0.209, 0.210 s |
| Instruction following | 190.31, 190.46 | 194.30, 194.50 | 0.211, 0.214 s |
| Long context | 169.27, 168.45 | 170.22, 170.69 | 0.462, 0.465 s |

The inline update is a repeatable 1–3% decode improvement on this host, but Garnet still trails vLLM's roughly 273 tok/s and its first-token latency by a wide margin. Keep `GARNET_GPT_OSS_INLINE_CONTROL_KERNEL=1` opt-in while concurrent-request safety and more workloads are checked. The short-prompt Garnet prefill values above are execution-only; vLLM's time-to-first-token also includes its scheduler and first decode token, so they are not identical boundaries. Raw Garnet JSON, logs and env files are in `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/garnet-{fused-lock-tile16,inline-fused-lock-tile16}/`.

An Nsight Systems capture of 32 arithmetic decode tokens with the batched-copy path recorded 55,744 CUDA kernels across both GPUs. GPU 0's captured span was 193.64 ms (6.05 ms/token under profiling, versus about 5.25 ms/token unprofiled). Per-token sums on GPU 0 were about 1.07 ms TensorRT GEMMs, 0.90 ms Marlin experts, 0.54 ms direct reductions, 0.23 ms split attention, 0.19 ms top-K routing, and 0.18 ms RMS normalization. These are kernel-duration sums, not additive wall-time shares. CUDA API events included 320 host-to-device copy calls and 160 stream synchronizations over the 32 tokens; their summed durations include thread overlap and profiler overhead. This motivated the inline control update, but its small end-to-end gain shows the GPU graph and the other host waits still dominate. The `.nsys-rep` and SQLite export are saved under `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/profiles/`.

The isolated, L2-flushed Marlin decode screen produced nine graph nodes and 0.7935 ms median for the original route, seven nodes and 0.7912 ms with fused routing, and six nodes and 0.7917 ms with fused routing plus lock clear. The node count fell, but this synthetic kernel timing does not show a meaningful latency gain. Retain the fusion flags as candidates until paired full-model A/B data supports them.

Paired arithmetic full-model runs with the inline control kernel isolated the other decode choices. With direct reduction and all other flags held fixed, no fusion gave **188.47 / 189.69 tok/s**, routing fusion alone **191.59 / 191.59**, and routing plus lock-clear fusion **193.84 / 192.86**. Thus the small full-model gain was real even though the isolated L2-flushed screen was nearly flat. With both fusions enabled, replacing direct reduction with NCCL yielded **187.06 / 187.94 tok/s**, versus **193.84 / 192.86** for direct reduction. Every arithmetic run gave the expected final JSON answer. Keep these variants opt-in; neither closes the vLLM gap, and the direct scratch implementation still needs concurrent-request safety work.

The long-prompt prefill A/B screen held the inline controls, direct reduction, fusions, and TensorRT cache fixed. Sixteen-query tiled attention took **0.462 / 0.465 s**; eight-query tiled attention took **0.468 / 0.469 s**; disabling tiled attention took **0.655 / 0.706 s**. All six runs gave the expected long-context answer. Tiling is clearly valuable on this 2,005-token request, while the sixteen-versus-eight difference is small and should remain a hardware-tuned choice. Even the faster tiled path is about four times the vLLM long-prompt time-to-first-token on this machine, with the timing-boundary caveat above.

A whole-process Nsight Systems vLLM worker trace was needed because a profiler API range set in its separate worker processes did not produce a report from the parent launch. The successful trace is saved locally as `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/profiles/vllm-arithmetic-fork.nsys-rep`, with a compressed SQLite export beside it. It shows vLLM using Marlin experts, `gemvx` BF16 matrix-vector kernels, one-stage cross-device reductions, attention, and compiled fused add/RMS operations. Its trace includes different startup and decode boundaries and heavier node-tracing overhead than Garnet's 32-token window, so do not compare their captured wall times directly. The kernel inventory points to reduction cost, pointwise fusion, and graph-launch gaps as next targets; paired end-to-end speed and answer checks remain the acceptance criterion.

The Workstation Edition attention-split screen used the best measured inline-control/direct-reduction/fused-route configuration on arithmetic. Eight splits yielded **187.41 / 187.98 tok/s**, sixteen splits **193.84 / 192.86**, and thirty-two splits **193.92 / 191.42**. The 32-split result varied enough that it does not justify changing the sixteen-split default; eight splits were slower. All six outputs had the expected final JSON, although generated token counts differed (94, 90, and 91 respectively), so more demanding numerical checks would be needed before claiming token-level equivalence. Raw runs are under `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/garnet-inline-split{8,32}-arith/` alongside the sixteen-split candidate above.

The inline-control Nsight capture of 32 arithmetic decode tokens contained **27,936 kernels on GPU 0** (873 per token), with **120.36 ms** of summed GPU 0 kernel time over a **188.31 ms** span. The difference, **67.95 ms / 32 = 2.12 ms per token**, includes graph-node scheduling gaps, cross-device dependencies, and overlap effects; it is not an independently removable host delay. The preceding batched-copy capture had 27,904 GPU 0 kernels, 120.51 ms summed kernel time, and a 193.64 ms span. Inline controls shortened the profiled span by about 5.3 ms over 32 tokens while leaving GPU kernel work nearly flat, consistent with the small full-model gain. The report and SQLite export are under `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/profiles/garnet-inline-fused-lock-tile16-arithmetic.*`. A vLLM worker trace shows fewer kernels per decode step, but its whole-process profiler overhead and capture boundaries differ; no traced-wall-time speed ratio is claimed.

An opt-in single-warp top-K route candidate kept each lane's four expert scores in registers through all eight selections, avoiding the four-warp reductions and repeated block barriers in the existing decoder router. It compiled on the target Blackwell GPUs and passed the full synthetic CUDA/TP2 suite, including Qwen compatibility. With all other candidate flags held fixed, the four saved pretrained prompts gave arithmetic **192.95 / 193.57**, code tracing **195.67 / 195.69**, instruction following **194.92 / 195.46**, and long-context **171.64 / 171.45 tok/s**. All eight expected final JSON answers passed. Warm long prefill was **0.463 s**. Against the saved no-warp baseline of **193.84 / 192.86**, **195.86 / 194.78**, **194.30 / 194.50**, and **170.22 / 170.69 tok/s**, these differences do not establish a useful throughput gain. The candidate was therefore reverted; raw logs, token IDs, decoded text, and environment records are under `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/garnet-inline-warp-topk/`.

A larger tiled-prefill KV stage (32 positions instead of 16) was tested with the existing sixteen-query tile. It compiled on Blackwell and passed the full synthetic/TP2 suite, including Qwen compatibility. On the saved 2,005-token request, the 32-position stage gave **0.470 / 0.461 s** prefill without fast exponentials, compared with **0.462 / 0.465 s** for the 16-position stage. With fast exponentials, the 32-position stage gave **0.453 / 0.455 s**, compared with **0.451 / 0.453 s** for the 16-position stage. All six final JSON checks passed across the 32-position and fast-exponential screens. Fast exponentials changed output length from 109 to 87 tokens despite matching the expected final answer, so they remain opt-in pending stronger numerical equivalence checks. The larger stage did not improve prefill and was reverted. Raw results are under `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/garnet-inline-{key32-long,key32-fast-long,fast-exp-long}/`.

A warm, second-pass Nsight Systems capture of the existing sixteen-query tiled long prefill took **0.325 s** at the application boundary (GPU spans 0.285/0.299 s). On GPU 0, summed kernel durations across 36 layers were **99.3 ms tiled attention**, **64.1 ms Marlin experts**, **52.7 ms NCCL all-reduces**, **36.9 ms router scores**, and **13.7 ms expert metadata**. GPU 1 had similar groups. These sums overlap, include profiler overhead, and use a second pass in the same process, unlike the first-pass prefill numbers above. They establish that no single kernel group can explain the entire long-prefill gap. The report and SQLite export are under `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/profiles/garnet-tiled16-long-prefill.*`.

An opt-in cuBLAS FP32 matrix multiply replaced the router-score CTAs for prefill requests with at least 128 tokens; decode routing was unchanged. On the saved long request it produced the expected final JSON twice, but prefill regressed to **0.520 / 0.510 s** from the roughly **0.46 s** tiled baseline. The direct plugin cuBLAS path also returned a TensorRT enqueue error only during a subsequent Nsight CUDA-profiler-range capture; the ordinary end-to-end runs succeeded. Because it was slower and the profiler path was fragile, the candidate was reverted. Raw results are under `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/garnet-router-sgemm-long/`. A future router redesign should benchmark the isolated operation and preserve the router's BF16 rounding and graph-capture behavior before full-model testing.

The attention projection is rounded to BF16 before TP all-reduce, so a new opt-in `GARNET_GPT_OSS_BF16_PREFILL_ALLREDUCE=1` path packs only that attention reduction to BF16 for requests of at least 128 tokens, performs NCCL BF16 sum, and unpacks to the existing FP32 tensor representation. MoE reduction and decode remain on their previous paths. TensorRT supplies per-invocation workspace for both BF16 buffers; plugin serialization version 7 distinguishes the new operator attribute. The rank-paired FP32/BF16 collective test and full synthetic/TP2 suite passed, including Qwen compatibility. All four pretrained cases passed twice with **identical generated token IDs** to the saved FP32 baseline, not just identical final JSON answers.

On the 2,005-token long prompt, three alternating warm BF16/FP32 process pairs on the same build gave BF16 **0.4586, 0.4589, 0.4582 s** versus FP32 **0.4622, 0.4628, 0.4650 s**. The median improvement was about **4.2 ms (0.9%)**. The full four-prompt BF16 run gave arithmetic **197.81 / 193.39**, code tracing **195.39 / 195.20**, instruction following **194.92 / 195.09**, and long-context decode **171.73 / 171.19 tok/s**; the collective choice affects prefill, not decode. Warm short-prefill measurements were around **0.208–0.213 s**, near the prior baseline. Long prefill had extra variability in later runs (**0.457 / 0.689 s**, then **0.455 / 0.494 s**), so the alternating A/B comparison is the narrower evidence for a small gain, not a guarantee on every invocation. Raw results are under `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/garnet-bf16-{ab-*,all-four,long-repeat,allreduce-long,control-long}/`.

A second-pass Nsight prefill capture with BF16 communication reduced GPU 0's summed NCCL FP32 kernels from **52.7 ms** over 72 calls to **29.1 ms** over 36 calls plus **12.5 ms** of BF16 NCCL over 36 calls; BF16 pack and unpack added **0.46 / 0.40 ms** of kernel time over 36 calls. The BF16 run's profiled application boundary was **0.312 s** versus **0.325 s** for the earlier FP32 second-pass capture, while GPU 0 span was **0.278 s** versus **0.285 s**. These profiles have overhead and must not be mixed with first-pass prefill numbers. Keep this path opt-in because the end-to-end benefit is modest and the full output/numerical equivalence has only been checked on the saved four requests. Reports and SQLite exports are under `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/profiles/garnet-{bf16,tiled16}-long-prefill.*`.

An opt-in GPT-OSS-only CUDA plugin then fused the attention residual add and following RMS normalization while writing the BF16-rounded residual required by the MoE branch. The kernel's two outputs matched the prior separate GPU operations exactly, and the full synthetic correctness suite passed, including TP2 and Qwen compatibility. With the other optimized flags fixed, all eight pretrained runs produced token sequences identical to the saved unfused baseline. Fused versus unfused warm wall decode was arithmetic **194.80/192.70 versus 193.84/192.86**, code tracing **195.05/194.81 versus 195.86/194.78**, instruction following **195.14/194.59 versus 194.30/194.50**, and long context **172.09/171.08 versus 170.22/170.69 tok/s**. Short prefill stayed around **0.21 s**; long prefill was **0.475/0.464 s**, with no repeatable gain over the roughly 0.46 s baseline. The extra plugin and TensorRT graph complexity did not justify these noise-level differences, so commits `dca0103` and `aea84c8` reverted the candidate. Raw logs and decoded outputs are in `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/garnet-fused-addnorm-all-four/`. This experiment reinforces that removing one or two simple kernels per layer is insufficient to close the roughly 75–100 tok/s decode gap; the next candidate should address a larger per-layer kernel group or a synchronization dependency.

Raising only the TensorRT builder optimization level from 1 to 5 on the same Workstation, with the other optimized flags fixed, gave arithmetic **197.74/197.25**, code tracing **198.12/198.79**, instruction following **198.14/198.65**, and long-context **174.83/174.11 tok/s**. All eight final-answer checks passed. However, arithmetic, instruction-following, and long-context token sequences changed relative to level 1, and the long-prefill values **0.473/0.462 s** did not improve on the earlier ~0.46 s. Fresh shape-specific engine compilation took several minutes. Keep level 1 as the default while comparing tactics and validating quality more broadly; the level-5 results are under `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54543362/garnet-trt-level5-all-four/`.

A related local candidate lets each decode router-score CTA clear its own Marlin lock slice (`GARNET_GPT_OSS_FUSED_MARLIN_LOCK_CLEAR=1`, effective only with fused routing). That removes the separate decode `cudaMemsetAsync` graph node while preserving the original path for prefill and for independent A/B comparison. The Windows RTX 4080 CUDA plugin built, and kernel parity passed both with both fusions enabled and with both disabled, including unsharded and two-rank Marlin cases. This is **not** a target-hardware speed result: writing the locks inside router CTAs could increase router duration enough to erase the saved launch. Benchmark the three configurations sequentially on the same GPU: no fusion, routing fusion only, and routing plus lock clearing. Run all four full prompts with final-answer checks before accepting either option.

A dedicated full-size decode-MoE CUDA-graph screen (`garnet_gpt_oss_marlin_decode_benchmark`) confirms the graph has **9 nodes** with no fusion, **7** with routing fusion, and **6** with routing plus lock clearing. On local RTX 4080, five sequential cycles of 25 L2-flushed graph replays per configuration gave trial medians of **1.073–1.081 ms** (baseline), **1.074–1.080 ms** (routing), and **1.072–1.095 ms** (routing plus locks). This does not establish an end-to-end gain: the differences are within local noise, the benchmark uses deterministic synthetic weights and routing, and the target is a Blackwell TP2 model. Raw timings are in `D:/CantorAI/work/vast-54530961/local-marlin-fusion-screen.txt`.

The installed vLLM 0.31.0 run selected FlashInfer XQA decode attention, CUDA graphs, and Marlin MXFP4 experts. The [current vLLM MXFP4 implementation](https://docs.vllm.ai/en/stable/api/vllm/model_executor/layers/quantization/mxfp4/) and [SGLang GPT-OSS model](https://github.com/sgl-project/sglang/blob/main/python/sglang/srt/models/gpt_oss.py) show hardware-specialized MoE and projection paths, including a FlashInfer tiny-GEMM path in SGLang on Hopper/Blackwell. These are design references for profiling and future experiments; the fused routing code here was written for Garnet and did not copy their source.

## Earlier Japan-machine end-to-end results

| Case | Optimized vLLM warm decode | Garnet with rank-local inputs and direct TP, warm wall decode | Status |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Arithmetic | ~248–249 tok/s | ~203–204 tok/s | Correct final answer |
| Code tracing | ~248–249 tok/s | ~203 tok/s | Correct final answer |
| Instruction following | ~248–249 tok/s | ~201 tok/s | Correct final answer |
| Long context | ~248–249 tok/s | ~177 tok/s | Correct final answer |

These Garnet numbers use the experimental direct reduction and rank-local inputs. The best local option remains roughly 18–19% behind vLLM on short requests and 29% behind on the long request. The goal is **not yet met**. Some full runs produce different output token counts despite passing the final-answer checks, so per-token timings and correctness are recorded separately.

vLLM time to first token was about 0.029–0.030 s for short prompts and 0.116 s for long context. Garnet's repeated prefill was about 0.050 s for short prompts and 0.446 s for long context with the 32-row Marlin prefill option; the opt-in fast-exponential trial reduced the repeated long prefill to about 0.439 s. The cold first pass was about 0.215 s for short prompts and 0.628 s for long context in the earlier run because it also packed weights. Garnet's prefill measurement and vLLM's time to first token do not cover exactly identical work, so this is a directional comparison rather than a claimed latency win.

## Decisions and tradeoffs

| Change or trial | Measurement and correctness | Decision / tradeoff |
|---|---|---|
| Persistent rank-local decode inputs | Alternating arithmetic A/B runs: old path **189.94, 190.91, 188.91 tok/s**; rank-local **199.59, 200.51, 199.82 tok/s**. All four final-answer checks passed. | Keep as opt-in `GARNET_TP_RANK_LOCAL_INPUTS=1` while validating on more workloads. It removes per-token GPU-1 input copies and allocations; it does not fix kernel or collective time. |
| Marlin prefill tile, 8 to 32 rows | Repeat short prefill ~0.055 to ~0.050 s; long ~0.505 to ~0.446 s. All four answers correct after adjusting shape-compatible column tiles. | Keep opt-in (`GARNET_GPT_OSS_MARLIN_PREFILL_BLOCK=32`, `GARNET_GPT_OSS_MARLIN_MAX_TOKENS=4096`). The first 256-column variant corrupted token 0, so output checking is mandatory. |
| Marlin 64-row prefill tile | Correct first token, but long prefill remained ~0.446 s. | Reverted: no measured gain over 32 rows. |
| Split each prefill query across 2 or 4 warps | On the same 2,005-token prompt, repeated prefill was **0.4464 / 0.4464 s** with the original kernel, **0.4496 / 0.4501 s** with 2 warps, and **0.4478 / 0.4475 s** with 4 warps. All variants produced the same first token (`200005`). | Reverted. Extra warp parallelism did not reduce the total work enough to beat the original one-warp-per-query kernel. The next prefill path needs tiling/data reuse or a tested fused-attention implementation. Raw trial logs are under `D:/CantorAI/work/gpt-oss-benchmark-evidence-2026-10-05/tp2-prefill-warp-df6bf28/`. |
| CUDA fast exponential in prefill softmax | On the same 2,005-token prompt and build, repeated baseline prefill **0.4463 / 0.4467 s** versus `__expf` **0.4390 / 0.4389 s**. The first token matched (`200005`), the CUDA parity suite passed with the option enabled, and all four full final-answer checks passed. | Keep opt-in as `GARNET_GPT_OSS_PREFILL_FAST_EXP=1`. This is only ~1.7% faster for long prefill. Full token sequences and output lengths can differ, despite matching expected final JSON, so broader numerical validation is needed before making approximate exponentials the default. Raw results are under `D:/CantorAI/work/gpt-oss-benchmark-evidence-2026-10-05/tp2-prefill-fastexp-19c13e1/` and `tp2-fastexp-19c13e1/`. |
| Eight-query tiled prefill attention, 16 staged KV positions | Local RTX 4080 kernel-only screening at 2,005 tokens and 32 query heads: full attention **20.50 → 10.80 ms/call**; 128-token sliding window **2.29 → 1.13 ms/call** (five timed calls after two warmups). The opt-in kernel compiled under WSL CUDA 12.0 and the CUDA parity suite passed, including a 40-token paged full/sliding test. | Candidate behind `GARNET_GPT_OSS_PREFILL_TILED_64=1` for head dimension 64. It reuses each shared-memory KV tile across eight adjacent queries; all warps keep their own online softmax and learned sink. This is a different GPU and an isolated kernel timing, so target Blackwell TP2 prefill and full-answer tests are still required before adopting it. The local benchmark harness is `D:/CantorAI/work/gpt-oss-prefill-tile-local-bench.cu`. |
| Sixteen-query tiled prefill variant | On the same local RTX 4080 synthetic shape, full attention **10.03 ms/call** versus **10.80 ms/call** with eight queries, while the 128-token sliding case regressed **1.13 → 1.23 ms/call** in the first screening. Both paged parity cases passed. A second 2,005-token run with 4,106,240 output elements per window found **99.9988% exact matches** to the untiled output for both tile sizes and both full/sliding windows; maximum absolute difference was one BF16 step (**0.00390625**), with none exceeding the parity tolerance. | Expose `GARNET_GPT_OSS_PREFILL_QUERY_TILE=16` only when tiled prefill is enabled; retain eight queries as the provisional default. Pick the tile on the actual hardware using the full model's alternating full/sliding layers, not the best isolated number. |
| Stage each paged KV offset once per 16-position tile | The original tiled loader recomputed the same page-table lookup for each of 64 head elements. Staging the 16 offsets in shared memory reduced the local RTX 4080 full-window 2,005-token kernel from **10.50 / 10.33 / 10.20** to **9.55 / 8.96 / 8.31 ms/call** in three paired runs (five calls per run). The 128-token sliding results were **1.31 / 1.27 / 1.33** versus **1.04 / 1.16 / 1.31 ms/call**, with more noise. On nonzero synthetic BF16 KV, the old and new tiled outputs matched; both matched the untiled output in 99.9988% of 4,106,240 elements per window, with maximum difference 0.00390625 and no elements outside the existing relative tolerance. CUDA parity passed with both eight- and sixteen-query tiles. | Keep the offset staging in the opt-in tiled kernel. The isolated measurements are on RTX 4080; rerun on Blackwell and measure full TP2 prefill and answer correctness before making a default choice. |
| Query tile follow-up with offset staging | Five paired 2,005-token RTX 4080 runs put eight queries at **8.90–9.58 ms** and sixteen at **8.51–9.04 ms** for full attention; 128-token sliding was **1.10–1.16 ms** versus **1.13–1.20 ms**. Three paired 4,096-token runs were **34.68–38.57 ms** versus **33.03–33.69 ms** full, and **2.15–2.46 ms** versus **2.03–2.26 ms** sliding. Each run timed five calls after two warmups. | Tile 16 is promising on this local GPU, especially for full attention, but leave the default at eight until the target Blackwell full-model A/B test. |
| TensorRT optimization level 3 and larger workspace | About 6.3% and 8.6% slower, respectively, in the measured trials. | Rejected; builder settings need measurement on this shape and hardware. |
| NCCL protocol overrides | `LL128` ~177.7 tok/s, `Simple` ~175.3 tok/s, versus ~200 tok/s with NCCL-selected protocol; `Tree` failed TensorRT `enqueueV3`. | Retain NCCL's selection. A protocol name alone does not predict end-to-end performance. |
| Direct two-GPU reduction, first scalar prototype | ~14.3 µs median kernel versus ~5.55 µs NCCL kernel in the same profiler trial. | Reworked; a custom collective is not inherently faster. |
| Direct two-GPU reduction, 2-CTA vectorized prototype | **NCCL 7.92 µs/op → direct 4.85 µs/op** for 1,000 standalone operations on this machine, with matching numerical output. Profiler medians over 200 operations: ~5.6 → ~4.45 µs/kernel. | This is a kernel-only result with preallocated input and peer buffers. The later staging-copy and model trials below give the relevant serving result. |
| Direct reduction with a device-to-device staging copy | Two 1,000-operation standalone runs: NCCL **7.96 / 7.89 µs/op**; direct plus copy **6.24 / 6.23 µs/op**. Outputs matched. | The copy consumed much of the original gain, but left ~1.7 µs/op in this microbenchmark. The later model trial below shows a smaller end-to-end gain. |
| Graph-captured direct reduction plus staging copy | One reduction per replay: NCCL **9.09, 8.47, 8.22 µs/op** versus direct **8.29, 8.22, 8.20 µs/op** in three trials, almost a wash. With 48 reductions captured in each graph and 100 replays: NCCL **7.36 / 7.34 µs/op** versus direct **5.76 / 5.73 µs/op** in two trials; outputs matched. | Host graph-launch overhead masks small kernels when each graph contains only one operation. The 48-operation graph is synthetic and repeats fixed inputs; the model trial below is the deciding measurement. |
| Direct reduction inside Garnet | Same build, rank-local inputs, paired sequential runs. Arithmetic NCCL **198.18 / 198.29 / 199.69** versus direct **204.22 / 203.72 / 202.95 tok/s** (warm wall). Code **198.52 → 202.62**, instruction **198.82 → 201.33**, long context **173.95 → 176.69 tok/s**. All four final answers passed. | Keep behind `GARNET_GPT_OSS_DIRECT_ALLREDUCE=1`. This is a modest 1–3% end-to-end gain, far smaller than the synthetic kernel gain. The shared staging area is only suitable for the current single-request, batch-1 decode trial; concurrent-request safety is not established. NCCL remains the default. |
| Batched decode control updates | The benchmark updated token, position, length, and slot scalars separately on each rank. `tensor_update_from_host` synchronized the CUDA stream after each copy, so this meant eight scalar-copy waits per generated token. The experimental `tensor_update_int_scalars` API acquires the four rank-local tensors together, enqueues four copies, and synchronizes once per GPU. The benchmark selects it with `GARNET_GPT_OSS_BATCH_CONTROL_UPDATES=1`. The Garnet C++ translation unit and runner syntax compiled locally. A CUDA-copy-only WSL RTX 4080 microbenchmark (`D:/CantorAI/work/gpt-oss-control-copy-bench.cu`) measured five 10,000-round trials: four separate copy/wait pairs **72.5–78.0 µs** versus four copies/one wait **25.1–27.2 µs** per rank. | This isolated result excludes XLang call overhead, tensor leases, TP2 execution, and the target GPU. Run the new scalar-copy correctness check and paired sequential full-model trials on the rented machine before claiming an end-to-end gain or changing the default. |

The direct reduction uses system-scope release/acquire signals, peer-access memory, and 128-bit vector reads. It is a hardware-specific candidate for small TP all-reduces, not a general NCCL replacement. The prototype lives in `D:/CantorAI/work/custom-allreduce-proto.cu`; the opt-in implementation is `plugins/gpt_oss/cuda/tp_direct.cu`. The measured model outputs and logs are in `D:/CantorAI/work/gpt-oss-benchmark-evidence-2026-10-05/tp2-direct-5a7a78d/` locally and `/workspace/CantorAI/work/tp2-direct-5a7a78d/` on the rented machine.

## Where time goes

A steady decode trace showed roughly 4.58 ms of GPU graph work per token plus ~0.66 ms between host graph launches. Per-GPU kernel groups were approximately TensorRT BF16 GEMMs 1.19 ms, Marlin experts 0.96–0.99 ms, NCCL 0.66–0.70 ms, attention 0.36 ms, and remaining operations ~1.07 ms. A faster collective helps only one group; reaching ~4.0 ms/token, the rough vLLM target, also requires reducing graph work and launch gaps. The long-context prefill gap is a separate problem.

A CUDA graph trace of the warmed 2,005-token prefill measured 0.465 s wall time under the profiler. After separating the second pass from the first pass's one-time repacking, the main per-GPU kernel totals were:

| Warm prefill group | GPU 0 | GPU 1 | Interpretation |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Attention | 239.9 ms | 237.4 ms | Largest cost; current prefill kernel walks prior keys serially per query warp. |
| NCCL all-reduce | 90.9 ms | 96.7 ms | Large hidden-state messages across PCIe, twice per layer. |
| Marlin experts | 59.2 ms | 56.2 ms | MXFP4 expert matrix products. |
| Router scores | 31.4 ms | 30.9 ms | Routing matrix/vector work. |
| Expert metadata | 16.4 ms | 16.6 ms | Sorting and padding expert assignments. |

These are kernel-duration sums on each GPU, not percentages of wall time; some work overlaps and profiler overhead affects latency. The complete trace and SQLite export are saved under `D:/CantorAI/work/gpt-oss-benchmark-evidence-2026-10-05/tp2-prefill-warm-profile/`. The attention loop in `plugins/gpt_oss/cuda/gpt_oss_kernels.cu` is the first prefill redesign target. Reducing that cost alone will not erase the collective and expert costs, so a vLLM-beating prefill still needs an end-to-end rerun.

Garnet's `xModel/gpt_oss/120b/profiles/tensorrt_mxfp4.json` describes backend/capability choices. The hardware planner in `tools/gpt_oss/pipeline.py` inspects available memory, reserves headroom, sizes KV cache, selects placement, and builds shapes. That is memory/placement fitting; the measured kernel tiles, TensorRT tactics, collective protocol, and graph behavior still need workload-specific profiling. Avoid encoding a single 2-GPU result as a universal default.

## Next checks

1. Keep the direct decode collective experimental until changing per-token activations, repeated requests, and concurrent-request safety are validated. Keep the separate BF16 prefill collective opt-in while testing more shapes and numerical outputs; its per-invocation workspace is safer, but it adds conversion kernels for a roughly 1% long-prefill gain.
2. Pursue a larger prefill redesign. The sixteen-query tiled path reduced warm 2,005-token prefill from roughly 0.66–0.71 s to 0.46–0.47 s, but optimized vLLM's long-prompt first-token time is about 0.116 s. Profile attention and large-message TP reductions together before changing the default.
3. Reduce decode graph work and launch gaps at the layer level. The warp-local top-K and scalar-update trials show that isolated micro-kernel wins cannot close a roughly 80 tok/s gap; the next candidate should remove multiple kernels per layer or a major collective/attention dependency while preserving BF16 behavior.
4. After each substantial change, rerun parity and all four saved pretrained cases, then compare against the same optimized vLLM baseline. Keep rejected results in this log so later hardware choices can be revisited with evidence.

Marlin-derived source attribution and Apache-2.0 terms are in the repository `NOTICE` and the GPT-OSS plugin provenance/license files. Qwen code is unchanged by this work.

## Batch throughput and workload profiles, October 7 UTC

The primary batch objective is maximum aggregate generated tokens per second. Each measured profile must retain GPU/topology and TP configuration, batch/concurrency, actual input and output lengths, maximum context reservation, KV dtype and allocated versus occupied bytes, resident/peak memory, prefill/first-token latency, decode throughput and complete-request throughput. Match actual token work and precision against vLLM. Input length affects prefill and initial KV use; output length grows KV and changes the contribution of decode to total time. Reserved KV capacity can be much larger than the measured request. A throughput maximum is specific to the tested shape and memory budget; heterogeneous lengths and continuous admission need separate validation.

At homogeneous batch32/input256/output128/context4096, the refreshed supported vLLM0.31 reference with an8192-token prefill budget gives **2907–2941 aggregate decode tok/s** across three independent complete-request trials. Garnet's corrected continuous-wall baseline gives **2596–2623**, inline vector updates **2637–2670**, and inline updates plus direct16 peer reduction **2760–2792** across three decode-only windows per configuration. All96 slot/trial expected answers and complete Garnet token sequences match the baseline. The combined median gain is about5.9%, but vLLM still leads; neither candidate has been promoted to a default.

Garnet prefill remains **2.71–2.73s**, producing only **959–974 execution-only complete-request output tok/s**, versus vLLM **2842–2886**. Garnet reserves **4608MiB BF16 KV/GPU**, while this workload fills about **431MiB** at completion. Sampled total usage reaches **97095MiB** on GPU0: lazy weight repacking makes the planner underestimate peak requirements. The additional **16.4–16.5s prefill/decode engine handoff is excluded** from execution throughput and must be solved for production latency. Complete evidence, timing boundaries, correctness and decisions are appended in [the research log](research/gpt_oss_120b_optimization_log.md).

### OPT-0050 design experiment: Separate full-history and sliding-window KV banks

**2026-10-07 22:27UTC, proposal BEFORE implementation.** Current model code alternates window128 on even layers and global attention on odd layers, but all36 BF16 KV layers reserve the complete context. The strict measured resident budget is close to its limit at B448 short and B144 long. This reduces the room available for batch-throughput exploration. The [vLLM0.31 attention-specific cache manager](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/vllm-project/vllm/v0.31.0/vllm/v1/core/single_type_kv_cache_manager.py) distinguishes full-attention retention from window-aware recycling. Treat it as architectural reference, not code to copy or proof of this proposed ring layout.

Design two shared rank-local KV banks:18 global layers retain all reserved context pages;18 sliding layers use a separately indexed page bank. Map physical bank layer as logical layer//2 and preserve original logical-layer weight/sink/window attributes. For window bank per request reserve page-round-up of **window + maximum prefilling query chunk -1**, not window alone: native writeKV writes every chunk token before attention reads, including padded-tail positions, so later writes can overwrite the earliest query's live history if the ring is too small. Window logical-page entries can repeat physical page IDs modulo the bank's pages-per-request; global logical tables and true positions/lengths remain unchanged. This must be proven against complete full-history positions through many wraps, ragged padded tails, next-token overwrite, inactive slots and repeated requests.

First implement only an independent CPU address/lifetime experiment outside runtime source: unique tagged positions detect aliasing, verify every causal window value against a full-history oracle, and include an intentionally undersized window-only ring that must fail. Separately calculate BF16 allocation and conditional admission from the measured engine bounds; these calculations cannot prove new TensorRT context bounds, ownership, FlashInfer masking/addressing or GPU quality. Runtime integration is still unimplemented and must remain disabled.

If the address experiment succeeds, propose default-off GPT-OSS-specific layout metadata/cache identity and explicit measured-profile selection. Extend the generic phase owner with an explicit tuple of all shared bank/table resources, both-phase exact object/device/layout checks and cleanup; preserve every default input/control/ownership path. Add only the extra model tensor arguments needed for window K/V/table, select the appropriate bank inside the GPT-OSS XModel, keep logical layer weights separate from physical bank layer, and require the complete alternating TP model rather than silently adapting unsupported stages. No Qwen change. Existing native page-offset and attention code appear structurally capable of separate bank tensors, but that is an inference from source inspection and requires native/compiled/full-model proof.

Required before use: independent CPU address oracle/negative ring control; original native attention vs FP64 including repeated physical page IDs/multiple bank layers/window boundaries/padding/active/uneven lengths, eager/changing graphs and unrestricted memory; full default/Qwen/API regressions; compiled both-bank shared ownership/refit/phase handoff/future-overwrite checks at existing numerical bounds; actual FlashInfer and scalar reference parity on repeated tables; regenerated binary/hardware/layout/cache matched profiles and sampled peaks including all bank/table bytes. Only then examine B512 short/B256 long as unvalidated shapes with fresh optimized Vfirst/Gsecond, all four exact inputs/output/context/KV/sampling and complete warmed requests. Analytic memory saving alone cannot advance accepted history or meet the speed goal. ### OPT-0052 proposal: Dedicated single-block XQA decode with complete-mask fallback

**Date/commit:** October8,2026 00:05UTC proposal before implementation; local feature/gpt-oss-120b at2df23c4. Current OPT48 parent2872767 remains live; no target sync/build or competing inference.

**Evidence/baseline:** Garnet's head64/GQA8 BF16 attention adapter currently uses FA2 BatchPrefill for both prefill64-query and decode16-query tiles, with complete causal/window/hole/active/capacity masking and sink injected once. The previously saved instrumented long B128 decode diagnosis places23.8% of GPU kernel time in FA2 attention; this is an older-shape diagnostic, not a current B144 timing bound. The current B144 complete scalar baseline2490.436638tok/s still trails supported optimized V3029.891493. OPT49 router and OPT50 banks are independently queued, without target proof. XQA is a hypothesis for a further decode reduction, not a substitute for those gates or a measured speed claim.

**Primary sources:** [FlashInfer XQA API](https://docs.flashinfer.ai/generated/flashinfer.xqa.xqa.html) documents BF16 paged decode, sinks and sliding windows. Inspected the installed0.7.0.post1 native `mha.h/mha.cu` and `jit/xqa.py`, then pinned source [v0.7.0.post1 mha.cu](https://github.com/flashinfer-ai/flashinfer/blob/v0.7.0.post1/csrc/xqa/mha.cu), commit946200de1ae94fc93fdd0926f0a13afd1fa7f0f1. The plain launch API can be called from the GPT-OSS native plugin without adding a Torch dependency. All borrowed files require original Apache2 headers/license, immutable upstream hashes and documented changes in a separate generated adapter.

**Design/hypothesis:** Add a default-off GPT-OSS decode-only native backend for head64, GQA8, BF16 NHD page16, tested high batches up to512 and logical context up to4096. Preserve the original public mask contract. Prepare normalized uint32 lengths, safe page indices, BF16 queries and a per-request safety/fallback mask on the enqueue stream. Dense valid rows use XQA; missing visible pages use the existing complete-mask scalar reference through a masked output-preserving fallback. Inactive/empty/over-capacity rows produce zero and cannot address invalid pages. Repeated physical window pages remain supported, subject to independent wrap/padding tests. Preserve query positions, true lengths, sink scaling and output dtype; do not shorten inputs or drop holes. Unsupported layouts/shapes keep the existing backend.

Use an explicit single-block launch specialization initially: the inspected upstream high-batch split rule selects one block when batch times local KV heads exceeds SM count, including these B144/B448 workloads. A forced single-block implementation removes semaphore/multi-block scratch requirements and preserves one sink normalization; this is a bounded candidate, not a general low-batch performance promise. No allocation or initialization inside captured enqueue. Replace upstream global CUDA initialization in a documented generated adapter with explicit per-device/context initialization before capture and checked statuses; namespace/prefix exported symbols. Include every query/output/normalized-table/length/fallback byte in workspace/admission, actual profile/native/cache identities and sampled peaks. Reject stale/unsupported identities.

**Required experiment:** Retain upstream bytes and hashes before adaptation; CPU dispatch/workspace/page-safety oracle and negative controls, compile-only where local hardware allows. Target native XQA vs independent FP64 scalar attention at existing bounds must cover sinks, global/window, dense/repeated/holes/invalid pages, inactive/empty/negative/over-capacity lengths, changing captured graphs and workspace lifetime. Default/Qwen/API, compiled cold/warm teacher-prefix/shared-KV, unrestricted sanitizer with actual app0 and strict XML audit, plus Nsight proof of XQA and fallback dispatch are mandatory. Only then generate new strict resident profiles and run all FOUR matched task prompts against optimized Vfirst, Gsecond, one complete warmup/three full requests, exact output/input/context/KV and full lifecycle. New batch shapes need fresh V references. Compare complete throughput/TTFT/decode/KV/peak/cold startup; changed greedy trajectories and routing confounds remain explicit. Decision PROPOSED; native backend and target proof not yet implemented, no accepted-history advancement.

### OPT-0058: Tile-based CPU Marlin code packing for faster validation cycles

**Date / System / Baseline:** October8 2026 08:42UTC proposal BEFORE prototype execution, branch afterdad5186. Current portable original host packer composes TP selection and eight nibble gathers per output word, calculating expert/tile coordinates per word; supported scales and explicit little-endian output are already covered by80 inverse-coordinate oracles. Prior prepacking saved GPU memory but increased fresh setup452.754 ->580.153s; current profile/model refit stages are costly. **Hypothesis / Proposed prototype:** Calculate expert/K/N tile coordinates once per128-word tile. Gather FOUR original bytes per word and permute their low/high nibbles into the identical Marlin v1 word, instead of loading each byte twice through eight nibble-coordinate selections. Keep scales/name/layout/shard/padding/fail-closed descriptors and little-endian stores unchanged. Tile work partitioning must retain the existing16-worker threshold in original WORD units, not accidentally serialize all ordinary tensors because the new work-unit count is128times smaller. This design records a possible next coherent change; it does not predict or claim target speed improvement.

**Hypothesis / Integration contract:** If the isolated selected-path native screen shows a useful repeatable cost reduction, add an explicit serialized default-OFF attention backend selector to GPT-OSS options and the plugin/lowering API. Selected decode requires the existing pinned XQA supported shape contract (single-token BF16 KV,64-dimension/GQA8/page16 and bounded batch/context/window); unsupported selected configurations fail closed. Retain the original attention implementation and every default XModel/Qwen behavior. Both selected/native arms must perform the same production KV write before attention; never substitute a read-only adapter or silently omit active-row/position handling. Candidate workspace is per-invocation,256-byte aligned and admitted from actual descriptors; no global mutable scratch or untracked runtime selector. **Context/capture ownership:** The installed NVIDIA NvInferRuntimePlugin.h explicitly says execution-context clone is not subsequently initialize()d and must already be initialized; attachToContext runs when execution resources are assigned. Therefore a selected plugin clone must own device-bound initialized XQA state before first graph capture, with checked errors and a valid current execution device. Context attachment/reassignment must check/reinitialize only outside capture; detach/terminate clear owned state. initialize(), clone() and attach lifecycle need actual compiled tests, including cached deserialization, cold/warm refit, independent overlapping-lifetime contexts and both GPUs. Enqueue must never perform CUDA attribute initialization, allocation or lazy hidden setup; missing/wrong-device state fails. Existing private adapter already checks current device and takes only invocation-owned scratch. This is an implementation constraint, not proof that the existing private native adapter is already integrated safely.

### OPT-0061: Native BF16 peer-transport candidate before inference selection

**Proposal before prototype edits:** Current measured all-four complete throughput still trails optimized vLLM by11-17%. Fresh source273 FAST0 arithmetic now has1536 validated measured answers and full trials7785.099987/7719.695946/7693.520164tok/s (median7719.695946 versus8766.219571); controller3927915 remains live in short code after warmup, so this is partial target observation, not downloaded whole-proof or a host-packing speed attribution. Do not pull/build or compete with the live target. Existing source/read-only historical traces support investigating communication and MoE, not reintegrating slower privateXQA.

**Evidence and hypothesis:** Historical source7e6e2fc long B128/chunk32 instrumented first/last prefill CSVs show Marlin summed-kernel-time47.2% each, NCCL BF16 allreduce24.5%/20.3%, and selected attention1.0%/7.9%. These are older shapes/binaries and overlapping two-GPU summed durations, NOT current critical-path fractions or promised attainable speedup. Current-shape bounded code/long traces remain queued. Garnet's current eligible BF16 collective always performs float-to-BF16 pack, NCCL SUM, then BF16-to-float unpack; existing direct peer path handles FP32 only and caps rows512. A private native BF16 peer path could reduce small/medium message protocol and conversion overhead, retaining pack plus a vectorized peer sum that writes rounded FP32 output. It might also support prefill3584/4096 rows, but large PCIe packets may be bandwidth-bound and slower; native paired evidence must decide. Preserve already-BF16 eligibility; expert partials remain ineligible. Do not silently compress arbitrary FP32 values, alter packet semantics or extend the unsafe whole8192-row Marlin kernel.

**Primary implementation research:** Read the ACTUAL installed vLLM0.31.0 custom_all_reduce.py and all_reduce_utils.py on Vast, without inference/source mutation. Its custom path accepts BF16/FP16/FP32, requires16-byte sizes/weak-contiguity, supports two PCIe ranks after actual P2P checks, and has an8MiB default message bound (SM120 absent from its architecture-specific table). Its captured path registers graph buffers. This is installed-source capability research, NOT proof which backend the saved vLLM requests executed. ### OPT-0070: Opt-in resident output reuse and native compact candidate merge

**Date / Commit / System:** October8 2026 20:56UTC, proposal BEFORE implementation; parent2c44ddb, target frozen48d136e, same TP2 Blackwell host. Local edits only; Qwen unchanged.

**Workload / Baseline:** Preserve four saved workloads B512/I256/O1024/C1280/chunk8 and B224/I2005/O1024/C3072/chunk16, true ragged tail, shared BF16 KV, exact optimized V references and one full warmup/three full requests. OPT68 group full rates7959.817/8013.173/8028.321/3595.625 versus V8766.220/9505.078/8836.099/4016.239. Existing strict measured admission and sampled memory limits remain mandatory.

**Hypothesis / Proposed Optimization:** A coherent default-off host-lifecycle candidate will (1) pass the already supported reuse_output request on each rank, retaining one output tensor per resident model; (2) merge the existing FP32 score/ID candidate pairs in a generic native CPU helper after the existing correctly ordered GPU-to-host observation, preserving score comparison, exact ID validation and minimum-ID ties; (3) omit host candidate sampling for intermediate prefill chunks while still executing the identical full engine/KV updates, and sample the true final chunk. These changes target observed allocations and interpreter work without changing a GPU kernel, precision, weight layout, true input length, or graph math. Measure the combined candidate, then attribute only with separate toggles if the gain is material. No claimed speedup yet.

**Implementation / Ownership Contract:** Separate explicit GARNET_RESIDENT_REUSE_OUTPUT, GARNET_RESIDENT_NATIVE_GREEDY_MERGE and GARNET_RESIDENT_FINAL_PREFILL_SAMPLE_ONLY flags, all default0, recorded in each result. Generic TensorParallel accepts explicit optional arguments; other XModels/default callers keep their behavior. Reused outputs are mutable observations and must be consumed before the next serial forward; existing model ownership/read-write leases and rank synchronization remain. Native merge accepts only materialized dense FP32 nonempty multiples of four and exact nonnegative IDs below2^24, keeps input read leases through the completed copy, returns fresh independent ID/value lists, and adds no GPU/pinned buffer or hidden asynchronous lifetime. Keep NaN-score branch behavior identical to the old comparator; no changed numerical tolerance or model oracle. Host controls/preparation/sampling/handoff/complete output assembly remain timed.

**Correctness / Performance Validation:** Independently compare native selections/score bits to finite order-statistic/tie oracles on CPU and each GPU, changing inputs, B1/224/512 and tails, exact ID edges and invalid shapes/dtypes/IDs; preserve source tensors. Run default/full API/Qwen/native/refit/compiled/resident gates plus unrestricted new-helper memory checks with actual apps0 and strict XML. Existing native/cache/group components can be reused only with explicit unchanged source/binary identity evidence, never silently called new tests. Fresh core binary requires new exact measured resident profiles/admission; verify reused-output model lifetimes/changed controls/cold-warm cache, all existing finite bounds and exact synthetic/pretrained trajectories. Once qualified, run matched supported optimized V FIRST or exactly revalidate its matching immutable references, then combined Garnet candidate sequentially on ALL FOUR complete requests, preserve cold/TTFT/KV/peak/latency/answer evidence. New performance accepted only from complete uninstrumented comparison; partial/diagnostic/kernel/decode wins do not meet the goal.

**Performance Result / Decision:** Pending; INCONCLUSIVE. No default enablement or accepted-history advance until independently downloaded serving evidence passes. Wider chunks/router numerical failures remain disabled; no continuous-scheduler or global max-capacity claim.

## OPT87: 2048-output long-retrieval result (2026-10-09)

The long batch was measured after the three fresh optimized vLLM 0.31.0 short references, using its own fresh vLLM long reference first and Garnet second. Both ran on the same two RTX PRO 6000 GPUs with batch128, the complete 2,005-token input, output cap2048, context4096 and prefill chunk16. The existing 11-row padded-prefill path selected the last valid token, retaining the true input length. Garnet used peer CTA128, the four explicitly enabled host lifecycle flags, prepacked resident TP2 engines and shared BF16/hybrid KV. One complete warmup preceded three complete timed requests; each request rewrote the input KV and included preparation, control updates, sampling, phase handoff and output assembly.

| Metric | Garnet | vLLM 0.31.0 | Garnet change / note |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Full-request median aggregate output | 4,244.26 tok/s | 4,667.79 tok/s | -9.07% |
| Full-request trial rates | 4,288.82 / 4,235.90 / 4,244.26 tok/s | 4,581.95 / 4,752.35 / 4,667.79 tok/s | all three retained |
| Decode-only median aggregate output | 5,590.32 tok/s | 4,676.79 tok/s | Garnet +19.54%; does not include full-request prefill cost |
| Median request first token | 14.848 s | 6.092 s | Garnet slower by 8.756 s |
| KV reservation per GPU | 10,003,415,040 bytes | 42,573,201,408 bytes | Garnet reserves less |
| Sampled peak per GPU | 79,167 / 79,149 MiB | 79,717 / 79,719 MiB | sampled, not exhaustive |
| Garnet cold startup | 169.279 s | recorded in raw reference | excludes module imports |

All384 generated responses per engine passed the saved long-retrieval answer check. ## OPT89 proposal BEFORE implementation: one mapped handshake per cooperative grid

2026-10-09 17:49 UTC. OPT88 long serving remains live on frozen source128914e; no target pull/build or competing GPU job. Current independently audited OPT84 first/last prefill traces show the owned BF16 peer reduction sums roughly39-45ms per GPU/chunk; these instrumented kernel sums are not wall-time shares or speed predictions. Current native source publishes and polls mapped-host start/end signals independently in every CTA, even though the launch is already cooperative and the entire pack precedes the reduce on one explicit owner stream. AtCTA128, one reduction performs128 start and128 end handshakes per rank.

Hypothesis: a default-off cooperative-grid signaling mode can use one mapped start/end handshake per rank instead of128, replacing block-local readiness with whole-grid barriers. Keep the existing BF16 conversion, rank0+rank1 operand order, vector reads, element assignment, output rounding, pack kernel, CTA count, serial phase contract, borrowed streams and retained owner resources. Do not compress FP32 expert partials. Preserve the original per-CTA kernel and select the candidate only through explicit validated0/1 GARNET_GPT_OSS_BF16_PEER_GRID_SIGNALS. The new owner must check occupancy for the actually selected cooperative kernel. All threads participate uniformly in grid barriers; publish pack-ready before any remote read, and publish completion only after every block has completed its peer reads. Retain timeout/wrap faults and fail closed on every observed fault. Keep arena accounting conservatively unchanged, add a distinct protocol/schema in the active descriptor and bind effective mode to cache/profile/kernel identity. Explicitly reject grid mode without enabled owned peer group. No default/Qwen change or performance claim.

NVIDIA's official CUDA Cooperative Groups guide requires cooperative launch for grid synchronization and participation by the entire group; it provides memory visibility across the synchronization boundary: https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/cuda-programming-guide/04-special-topics/cooperative-groups.html . The candidate remains a hypothesis: three device grid barriers can cost more than the removed mapped polling, and altered scheduling can expose a lifetime/order defect. First validate independent CPU-bit reduction and changing-input/output-tail/retained-graph/phase/release-reacquire fixtures atCTA64/128/188, all default native/Qwen/API/refit gates and unrestricted strict sanitizer XML (unchanged exact exclusions/bounds). Preserve actual apps/exits/raw matrices/failures. Only after the current owner is terminal and all downloaded evidence saved may target pull/build and gate this source. Then exact current-native short/long profiles and strict measured admission, optimized latest stable vLLM references first and Garnet second, sequentially, one complete warmup/three complete timed requests with full input KV rewrite. Compare all four full-request throughput, TTFT/prefill/decode, cold startup/KV/peaks and every trajectory with the qualified same-shape control. Test the fastest admitted1024-output profiles first; the completed2048-output shape screens do not prove maximum capacity/throughput. No selection ACCEPT unless full measured objective and correctness are met. Spend cap250USD including bandwidth,88h cutoff and15GiB disk floor remain.

**OPT93 local native-state implementation prepared, actual 2026-10-09T21:39:18Z:** Added a generic bounded CPU `GreedyBatchState` and XLang native API for create/step/history/release. It preserves the existing FP32 score/ID comparison (including NaNs and minimum-ID ties), validates IDs and request dimensions, stores token history in native memory, returns one by-value feedback ID vector for the existing rank-worker integer-pattern path, and caps live handles at eight. GPT-OSS resident runner selection is explicit/default-off (`GARNET_RESIDENT_NATIVE_GREEDY_STATE=0`), mutually exclusive with the existing native merge candidate; the default no-state/no-scheduling path keeps its previous timer call sequence. Complete-request records now distinguish native state release and wrapper-finalization time, and the independent auditor binds the effective environment to saved host-candidate fields and per-trial state flags. The final-step token accessor remains valid for output length one; no current/history storage pointer escapes.

Local checks: WSL C++17 `-Wall -Wextra -Werror` host-state test passes across B=1/7/224/512, 76 malformed/stale/released cases, exact ties/NaN behavior, immutable inputs, concurrent by-value snapshots, reset and release. Python syntax plus runtime-scheduling, resident host lifecycle and controller-environment tests pass; `git diff --check` passes. The local Windows CMake build reached existing XLang3 sources but failed before compiling the Garnet target because `xlang3/src/runtime/modules/system/sre_module.cpp` uses unavailable MSVC `std::regex_constants::multiline`; this is not target compile evidence. Actual target core build, native API/GPU contract, complete strict sanitizer/Qwen/default gates, and full four-prompt V-first/G-second unprofiled serving comparison are still required. No performance result or accepted-history advance is claimed. The separate OPT92 private peer-push candidate remains rejected by its independently audited +0.27% B512 and +2.82% B4096 costs; production binaries and Qwen remain unchanged.
